This ring is to $\mathbb{Z}_4$ what the field $\mathbb{F}_8$ is to $\mathbb{Z}_2$.  It has $64$ elements, reduction modulo two maps it onto $\mathbb{F}_8$, and its unit group contains a unique cyclic subgroup of order $2^3-1=7$, generated by a root $\xi$ of $h$.  The powers of $\xi$ together with $0$ form the Teichm\"uller set, a canonical set of representatives of the residue field $\mathbb F_8$, closed under multiplication but not generally under addition, and $\xi$ is called a Teichm\"uller unit.  For $q=4$, the anyons created at the ends of $Z$-type strings, i.e., the configurations of violated vertex terms modulo those created by single $Z$ errors, form the module $\mathbb{Z}_4[x^{\pm1},y^{\pm1}]/(v(x),\bar v(y))$ with $\bar v(y)=v(y^{-1})$, where $x$ and $y$ act as the two translations~\cite{Liang2024extracting}.  Since $v=-h$, this module is $\mathrm{GR}(4,3)[y]/(\bar v(y))\cong\mathrm{GR}(4,3)^3\cong\mathbb{Z}_4^9$, with $x$ acting as $\xi$ and with $\bar v$ splitting into three linear factors with roots $\xi^{6},\xi^{5},\xi^{3}$.  The logical string operators have a nondegenerate commutation pairing, so the anyons braid as in nine copies of the $\mathbb{Z}_4$ toric code.  A translation by one site along $x$, however, multiplies every anyon charge by $\xi$.  Since $\xi-1$ is a unit of $\mathrm{GR}(4,3)$, no nontrivial anyon returns to itself under fewer than seven translations, while $\xi^7=1$ makes translation by seven sites act trivially, which is why the code is trivial unless $7\mid L$.  Translations along $y$ act as $\xi^{6}$, $\xi^{5}$, and $\xi^{3}$ on the three factors of the charge module, so each factor is invariant along exactly one of the directions $(1,1)$, $(2,1)$, and $(4,1)$, whose first components $1,2,4$ are the quadratic residues modulo seven.  This is the mechanism of Wen's plaquette model~\cite{Wen2003plaquette}, in which a translation exchanges the $e$ and $m$ anyons of the toric code, except that here the permutation has order seven instead of two.  Coarse-graining by seven sites removes the permutation and leaves nine decoupled toric codes.  The same algebra applies for larger $q=2^r$ with $\mathrm{GR}(q,3)$ in place of $\mathrm{GR}(4,3)$, so we expect nine $\mathbb{Z}_q$ toric codes with the same order-seven action for every $q=2^r$, although we verified the braiding only at $q=4$.  The same construction accepts the lifted Golay code of Example~\ref{eg:liftedgolay} in place of the lifted Hamming code, on tori with $23\mid L$, where it yields $121$ toric codes enriched by translations of order $23$.
<!-- end eg-13 -->
<!-- defn-8: body_md -->
Pick two natural numbers $a>b\ge 1$ with $\gcd(a,q)=\gcd(b,q)=\gcd(a,b)=1$. The $(a,-b)$ helical repetition code with open boundary conditions (OBC) has parity-check matrix $H_{\mathrm{OBC}}\in\mathbb{Z}_q^{(n-1)\times n}$: 
\begin{equation}
        H_{\text{OBC}}(a,-b):= \left(\begin{array}{cccccc} a &\ -b &\ 0 &\ \cdots &\ 0 &\ 0 \\ 0 &\ a &\ -b &\ \cdots &\ 0 &\ 0 \\ \vdots &\ \vdots &\ \vdots &\ \ddots &\ \vdots &\ \vdots  \\ 0 &\ 0 &\ 0 &\ \cdots &\ a &\ -b\end{array}\right).
    \end{equation}
    The helical repetition code with periodic boundary conditions (PBC) has parity-check matrix $H_{\mathrm{PBC}}\in \mathbb{Z}_q^{n\times n}$, which adds one extra parity check at the bottom: 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:helicalPBC}
        H_{\text{PBC}}(a,-b):= \left(\begin{array}{cccccc} a &\ -b &\ 0 &\ \cdots &\ 0 &\ 0 \\ 0 &\ a &\ -b &\ \cdots &\ 0 &\ 0 \\ \vdots &\ \vdots &\ \vdots &\ \ddots &\ \vdots &\ \vdots  \\ 0 &\ 0 &\ 0 &\ \cdots &\ a &\ -b  \\ -b &\ 0 &\ 0 &\ \cdots &\ 0 &\ a \end{array}\right).
    \end{equation}
<!-- end defn-8 -->
<!-- prop-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:helicalproperties}
    The codespace of the helical repetition code with open boundary conditions is \begin{equation} \label{eq:helical_codeword}
        \mathcal{C} = \mathrm{span}\left\lbrace \left(\begin{array}{cccc} b^{n-1} &\ ab^{n-2} &\ \cdots &\ a^{n-1} \end{array}\right) \right\rbrace .
    \end{equation}
     These are also the codewords with periodic boundary conditions if $a^n=b^n \text{ (mod $q$)}$. If $c=\gcd(q,a^n-b^n)>1$, then $q/c$-multiples of the generator in \eqref{eq:helical_codeword} remain valid codewords.
     For sufficiently large $q$, the Lee distance of this code grows exponentially: $d>a^{n-1}$.
<!-- end prop-8 -->
<!-- prop-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:helicalPBCsound}
Let $H\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}$ be invertible over $\mathbb{Q}$, and let $q=|\det H|\geq 2$. The corresponding code over $\mathbb{Z}_q$ contains exactly $q$ distinct codewords.
For $\mathbf{z}_0\in\mathbb{Z}_q^n$, define its reduced distance to the codespace by
\begin{equation}
    d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C})
    :=\min_{\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}}
    |\mathbf{z}_0-\mathbf{c}|_{\mathrm{L}}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:invertibleH_soundness}
    \frac{d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C})}
         {\|H^{-1}\|_{1\to1}}
    \leq |H\mathbf{z}_0|_{\mathrm{L}}
    \leq \|H\|_{1\to1}
         d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C}).
\end{equation}
<!-- end prop-9 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Let $s_1,\ldots,s_n$ be the positive Smith invariant factors of $H$. Since $\prod_{i=1}^n s_i=|\det H|=q$, each $s_i$ divides $q$. Hence, by Proposition~\ref{prop:classicalcodespace},
\begin{equation}
    |\mathcal{C}|
    =\prod_{i=1}^n\gcd(s_i,q)
    =\prod_{i=1}^n s_i
    =q.
\end{equation}
Fix $\mathbf{z}_0\in\mathbb{Z}_q^n$, and choose a nearest codeword $\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}$. Let $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^n$ be the centered integer lift of $\mathbf{z}_0-\mathbf{c}$, with entries in $(-q/2,q/2]$, so that $|\mathbf{x}|_1=|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}$. Then
\begin{equation}
    |\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}
    =|\mathbf{z}_0-\mathbf{c}|_{\mathrm{L}}
    =d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C}).
\end{equation}
Since $H\mathbf{c}=0$ mod $q$, the upper bound follows from
\begin{equation}
    |H\mathbf{z}_0|_{\mathrm{L}}
    =|H\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}
    \leq \|H\|_{1\to1}|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}
    =\|H\|_{1\to1}d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C}).
\end{equation}

For the lower bound, let $\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^n$ be the centered representative of $H\mathbf{x}$ mod $q$, so that
\begin{equation}
    r_i\in(-q/2,q/2]\cap\mathbb{Z}.
\end{equation}
There exists $\mathbf{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^n$ such that
\begin{equation}
    H\mathbf{x}=q\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{r}.
\end{equation}
By construction,
\begin{equation}
    |\mathbf{r}|_1=|H\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}=|H\mathbf{z}_0|_{\mathrm{L}},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    \mathbf{x}=qH^{-1}\mathbf{s}+H^{-1}\mathbf{r}.
\end{equation}
Note that because we chose $q=|\det(H)|$, $qH^{-1}$ is an integer matrix, meaning that $H^{-1}\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^n$. Since $H(qH^{-1}\mathbf{s})=q\mathbf{s}$, the first term reduces modulo $q$ to a codeword. Since $\mathbf{x}$ is already the minimum displacement from the codespace, we have
\begin{equation}
|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}\leq|H^{-1}\mathbf{r}|_{\mathrm{L}}\leq|H^{-1}\mathbf{r}|_1\leq \lVert H^{-1}\rVert_{1\to 1}|\mathbf{r}|_1=\lVert H^{-1}\rVert_{1\to 1}|H\mathbf{z}_0|_{\mathrm{L}}.
\end{equation}
This proves the lower bound.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- cor-3: body_md -->
Consider the $(a,-b)$ helical repetition code with periodic boundary condition defined on $\mathbb{Z}_q$ on a one-dimensional chain of length $n$. Let
    \begin{equation}
        C=a+b,\quad  \kappa=|a-b|.
    \end{equation}
The codespace contains exactly $q$ distinct codewords, with 
\begin{equation}
    q=|\det(H)|=|a^n-b^n|.
\end{equation} 
Then every configuration $\mathbf{z}_0\in \mathbb{Z}^n_q$ satisfies
    \begin{equation} \label{eq:PBC_linearconfinement}
        \kappa d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C})\leq|H\mathbf{z}_0|_{\mathrm{L}}\leq Cd_{\rm r}(\mathbf{z}_0,\mathcal{C}).
    \end{equation}
<!-- end cor-3 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
The parity-check matrix of the $(a,-b)$ PBC helical code can alternatively be written as
    \begin{equation}
        H=aI-bS,
        \label{eq:helicalH}
    \end{equation}
    where $S$ is the cyclic shift matrix with $S^n=I$. Then 
    \begin{equation}
       \lVert H\rVert_{1\to1}=a+b=:C, \quad |\det(H)|=|a^n-b^n|=:q,
    \end{equation}
    which means that $H$ is invertible over $\mathbb{Q}$. The code is therefore confined and sound. The inverse matrix
    \begin{equation}
        H^{-1}=\frac{1}{a^n-b^n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a^{n-1-k}b^k S^k.
    \end{equation}
    Since each $S^k$ is a permutation matrix, there is only a single 1 in each column. Therefore, the maximum column weight is
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert H^{-1} \rVert_{1\to1}=\frac{1}{|a^n-b^n|}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a^{n-1-k}b^k=\frac{1}{|a-b|}=:\kappa^{-1}.
    \end{equation}
Proposition~\ref{prop:helicalPBCsound} immediately gives
Eq.~\eqref{eq:PBC_linearconfinement}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- prop-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:PBCslowmixing}
Consider the PBC $(a,-b)$ helical repetition code defined on $\mathbb{Z}_q$ on a one-dimensional chain of length $n$. Set $q=a^n-b^n\ge2$, $C=a+b$, and $\kappa=a-b$. Choose a Lee ball of radius $R$ satisfying
    \begin{equation}
        R=\lfloor\frac{q}{2C}-\frac{1}{2}\rfloor.
    \end{equation}
    Under the single-site Metropolis dynamics, in which each step changes one site by $+1$ or $-1$, the memory time satisfies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:PBCtmix}
        t_{\text{mem}}(1/4)\gtrsim\frac{1}{8}\frac{(n-1)!}{(2R)^{n-1}}\exp\left(\beta\kappa R\right).
    \end{equation}
For finite $n$, this bound defines a finite-size inverse-temperature threshold $\beta_c$. For $\beta>\beta_c$, the memory time is exponentially large in $q$, with $\beta_c$ approximately determined by
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:PBCT_c}
        \beta_c\kappa R\approx (n-1)\log{2R}-\log{(n-1)!},
    \end{equation}
    which gives 
    \begin{equation}
        \beta_c=O(n^2/q).
    \end{equation}
In particular, for any fixed $\beta>0$, the memory time is exponentially large in $q$.
<!-- end prop-10 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We first show that the distance $d$ of the PBC helical code is lower bounded by
    \begin{equation}
        d\geq \frac{q}{C}.
    \end{equation}
    Take the centered lift $\mathbf{r}$ of a nonzero codeword $\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}$ with $r_i\in(-q/2,q/2]\cap\mathbb{Z}$ such that $|\mathbf{c}|_{\mathrm{L}}=|\mathbf{r}|_1$. Since $H$ is invertible, $H\mathbf{r}=q\mathbf{s}$ with $\mathbf{s}\neq0$. Thus
    \begin{equation}
    q\leq|H\mathbf{r}|_1\leq(a+b)|\mathbf{r}|_1=C|\mathbf{c}|_{\mathrm{L}}.
    \end{equation}
    Choose a ball of radius $R=\lfloor\frac{q}{2C}-\frac{1}{2}\rfloor$,
    \begin{equation} \label{eq:PBCballprooftwo}
        B=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^n_q:|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}\leq R\}.
    \end{equation}
    Since the code distance for the PBC helical code is lower bounded by $q/C$, the balls $B$ and $B+\mathbf{v}$ are disjoint for any nonzero codeword $\mathbf{v}$.                      
    Every state $\mathbf{x}\in B$ satisfies 
    \begin{equation}
        |\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}=d_{\rm r}(\mathbf{x},\mathcal{C}),
    \end{equation}
    and has energy bounded by
    \begin{equation} \label{eq:PBCconfprooftwo}
        \kappa|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}\leq|H\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}\leq C|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}.
    \end{equation}
    Consider the stationary probability flow
    \begin{equation}
        Q(B,B^c):=\sum_{\substack{\mathbf{x}\in B \\ \mathbf{y}\notin B}}\pi(\mathbf{x})P(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),
    \end{equation}
    which counts the probability flow across the boundary $\partial B=\{\mathbf{x}:|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}=R\}$. From the soundness of the code, we obtain
    \begin{align}
        Q(B,B^c)&\leq \sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\partial B}\pi(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{\mathbf{x}:|\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}=R}e^{-\beta|H\mathbf{x}|_{\mathrm{L}}}\\
        &<2^n\binom{n+R-1}{n-1}\frac{e^{-\beta\kappa R}}{Z}=\frac{2^n R^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\left(1+\frac{j}{R}\right)\frac{e^{-\beta\kappa R}}{Z}\\
        &\approx\frac{2^n R^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\frac{e^{-\beta\kappa R}}{Z}, \label{eq:boundary flow}
    \end{align}
    where the second inequality follows from the crude counting bound on $|\partial B|$ obtained by choosing nonnegative weights summing to $R$ and assigning at most two signs to each site. And the last approximation follows from $R=O(\exp(n))$.

We next convert the stationary probability flow across the boundary of $B$ into a bound on the
memory time for a chain initialized at the zero codeword~(cf.\ Ref.~\cite{Chesi2010thermodynamic} for a quantum analogue). Let $X_s$ denote the configuration after $s$
updates, with $X_0=\mathbf{0}$, and let $P^s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})$ denote the corresponding $s$-step transition probability.
Reversibility implies that
\begin{equation}
    \pi(\mathbf{x})P^s(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
    =
    \pi(\mathbf{y})P^s(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})
\end{equation}
for every $s\ge 0$. Taking $\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}$ and using $P^s(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{0})\le 1$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
    P^s(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{y})
    =
    \frac{\pi(\mathbf{y})}{\pi(\mathbf{0})}
    P^s(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{0})
    \le
    \frac{\pi(\mathbf{y})}{\pi(\mathbf{0})}.
\end{equation}
It follows that the probability of crossing from $B$ to $B^c$ between steps $s$ and $s+1$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    \mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{0}}(X_s\in B,\;X_{s+1}\notin B)
    &=
    \sum_{\substack{\mathbf{x}\in B\\ \mathbf{y}\notin B}}
    P^s(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{x})P(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}) \\
    &\le
    \frac{1}{\pi(\mathbf{0})}
    \sum_{\substack{\mathbf{x}\in B\\ \mathbf{y}\notin B}}
    \pi(\mathbf{x})P(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}) =
    \frac{Q(B,B^c)}{\pi(\mathbf{0})}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
    \tau_{B^c}
    =
    \inf\{s\ge 0:X_s\notin B\}
\end{equation}
be the first time at which a given trajectory leaves $B$. If $\tau_{B^c}\le t$,
then the trajectory must cross from $B$ to $B^c$ during one of the first $t$
steps. A union bound therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    \mathbb P_{\mathbf 0}(\tau_{B^c}\le t)
    &\le
    \sum_{s=0}^{t-1}
    \mathbb P_{\mathbf 0}(X_s\in B,X_{s+1}\notin B)
    \\
    &\le
    t\,\frac{Q(B,B^c)}{\pi(\mathbf 0)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

We can choose a decoder such that every configuration in $B$ is decoded to the zero codeword. 
The decoding-failure probability therefore
satisfies
\begin{equation}
    p_{\mathrm{fail}}(t)
    \le
    \mathbb P_{\mathbf 0}(X_t\notin B)
    \le
    \mathbb P_{\mathbf 0}(\tau_{B^c}\le t)
    \le
    t\,\frac{Q(B,B^c)}{\pi(\mathbf 0)}.
\end{equation}
Thus $p_{\mathrm{fail}}(t)<1/4$ for every integer $t$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
    t
    <
    \frac{\pi(\mathbf 0)}{4Q(B,B^c)},
\end{equation}
which yields
\begin{equation} \label{eq:memorytimebound}
    t_{\mathrm{mem}}(1/4)
    \ge
        \frac{\pi(\mathbf 0)}{4Q(B,B^c)}.
\end{equation}

We now combine this estimate with the boundary-flow bound obtained in \eqref{eq:boundary flow}. Together with $\pi(\mathbf 0)=1/Z$, the memory time is therefore lower bounded by \eqref{eq:PBCtmix}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- prop-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:PBCOBCHGP}
Fix two pairs of coprime positive integers
\begin{equation}
    a_h>b_h\ge 1,
    \qquad
    a_v>b_v\ge 1.
\end{equation}
Let $H_h=H_{\mathrm{PBC}}(a_h,-b_h)\in \mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}$ be the parity-check matrix of a helical
repetition code with periodic boundary conditions, and let $H_v=H_{\mathrm{OBC}}(a_v,-b_v)\in \mathbb{Z}^{(n-1)\times n}$ be the parity-check matrix of a helical repetition code with open boundary conditions. 
Assume that $q$ satisfies
\(
    a_h^n \mathrel{\mathchar"3211} b_h^n \pmod{q}
\)
and
\(
    \gcd(q,a_hb_ha_vb_v)=1.
\)

Construct the CSS hypergraph-product code over $\mathbb Z_q$ with check matrices
\begin{equation}
    H_X =
    \left(
    H_h\otimes I_n
    \,\middle|\,
    I_n\otimes H_v^{\mathsf T}
    \right), \quad
    H_Z =
    \left(
    -I_n\otimes H_v
    \,\middle|\,
    H_h^{\mathsf T}\otimes I_{n-1}
    \right).
\end{equation}
Then $H_XH_Z^{\mathsf T}=0$, so this defines a CSS code over $\mathbb Z_q$ on
\(
    N=2n^2-n
\)
physical qudits.

Consider the single-site Metropolis dynamics on $Z$-error configurations, in which each time step changes one coordinate by $+1$ or $-1$. There exist constants
$\kappa_h,\eta,\rho>0$, depending only on $a_h,b_h,a_v,b_v$, such that the memory time satisfies
\begin{equation} \label{eq:tmixPBCOBCHGP}
    t_{\mathrm{mem}}(1/4)
    \ge
    \frac{1}{4}
    \exp\!\left[
        \beta\bigl(\kappa_h( \rho q-1) - \eta n(n-1)\bigr)
        -N\log\!\bigl(2( \rho q+N)\bigr)
    \right].
\end{equation}
In particular, for any fixed inverse temperature $\beta>0$, along any family of
parameters satisfying
\begin{equation}
    q\gg n^2\log (q+n),
\end{equation}
the memory time in the $Z$-error sector is exponentially large.
<!-- end prop-11 -->
<!-- prop-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:classicalOBC}
Fix coprime integers $a>b\ge 1$. For each $n$, consider the length-$n$
open-boundary $(a,-b)$ helical repetition code over $\mathbb Z_q$, defined by
the parity-check matrix
$H_{\mathrm{OBC}}(a,-b)\in \mathbb Z_q^{(n-1)\times n}$.
Let
\begin{equation}
    \pi(x)\propto \exp\bigl(-\beta |H_{\mathrm{OBC}}(a,-b)x|_{\mathrm{L}}\bigr),
    \qquad
    x\in \mathbb Z_q^n,
\end{equation}
and consider the corresponding Metropolis dynamics, in which each time step changes one coordinate by $+1$ or $-1$.

Then there exist positive constants $c,C>0$, depending only on $a$ and $b$,
such that for all sufficiently large $n$, whenever
\begin{equation}
    q
    \ge
    Cn\max\left\{
        1,\,
        \frac{1}{\beta}\log\left(2+\frac{1}{\beta}\right)
    \right\},
\end{equation}
the memory time satisfies
\begin{equation}
    t_{\mathrm{mem}}(1/4)\ge \exp(cn).
\end{equation}
In particular, this family of codes has exponentially large memory time at every finite temperature whenever $q$ grows superlinearly with $n$.
<!-- end prop-12 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The complete proof of the proposition is given in
Appendix~\ref{app:proofclassicalOBC}. Here, we only describe the geometric
picture underlying the proof. When the model is lifted to $\mathbb Z$, the kernel
of $H_{\mathrm{OBC}}(a,-b)$ is generated by the integer codeword
\begin{equation}
    v=\left(b^{n-1},ab^{n-2},\ldots,a^{n-1}\right).
\end{equation}
Taking $\mathbb Rv$ as the axis and an $O(q)$-radius neighborhood in the
transverse directions defines a cylinder in $\mathbb Z^n$. We take the length of the cylinder equal to that of $v$, so that the cylinder contains exactly one codeword.

Translation along the axis leaves the syndrome unchanged, so the energy is
completely flat in this direction. By contrast, the energy grows at least
linearly with the distance from the axis, producing linear confinement in the
transverse directions. The side of the cylinder therefore has energy of order
$q$, while the bulk extends along the energy-flat axis.

Reducing each coordinate modulo $q$ folds the cylinder into $\mathbb Z_q^n$.
The cylinder can be chosen sufficiently narrow that it does not self-intersect
under this reduction and retains the energy landscape over $\mathbb Z$. Its
boundary consists of two caps and the transverse side. Physically, a state
inside the cylinder has only two possible escape routes. It can travel along
the low-energy axis toward a different codeword, but this direction remains
exponentially long even after folding, so reaching a cap under the local
dynamics takes an exponentially long time. Alternatively, it can escape through the
transverse side, but linear confinement forces it to cross a large energy
barrier. 
Therefore, for any fixed $\beta>0$, when $q$ satisfies the conditions of Proposition \ref{prop:classicalOBC}, the memory time is exponentially large.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- prop-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:OBCOBCHGP}

Fix two pairs of coprime positive integers
\begin{equation}
    a_h>b_h\ge 1,
    \qquad
    a_v>b_v\ge 1,
    \qquad
    a_h>a_v.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
    H_h=H_{\mathrm{OBC}}(a_h,-b_h)\in\mathbb Z^{(n-1)\times n},
    \qquad
    H_v=H_{\mathrm{OBC}}(a_v,-b_v)\in\mathbb Z^{(n-1)\times n}
\end{equation}
be the parity-check matrices of OBC helical repetition codes. Assume
that
\begin{equation}
    \gcd(q,a_hb_ha_vb_v)=1
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    q>2(a_h+b_h+a_v+b_v).
\end{equation}
Construct the CSS hypergraph-product code over $\mathbb Z_q$ on
\(
    N=n^2+(n-1)^2           
\)
physical qudits with check matrices
\begin{equation}
    H_X=
    \left(
        H_h\otimes I_n
        \,\middle|\,
        I_{n-1}\otimes H_v^{\mathsf T}
    \right),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
    H_Z=
    \left(
        -I_n\otimes H_v
        \,\middle|\,
        H_h^{\mathsf T}\otimes I_{n-1}
    \right).
\end{equation}

Consider the nearest-neighbor single-site Metropolis dynamics on $Z$-error
configurations, in which each time step changes one coordinate by $+1$ or $-1$,
with energy
\begin{equation}
    E_Z(z)=|H_Xz|_{\mathrm{L}}.
\end{equation}
Then there exist positive constants $c,\gamma,B >0$,
depending only on $a_h,b_h,a_v,b_v$, such that
\begin{equation} \label{eq:tmixOBCOBCHGP}
    t_{\mathrm{mem}}(1/4)
    \ge
    c\min\left\{
        \mathrm{e}^{\gamma n},
        \exp\!\left[
            \beta\left(\frac{q}{2}-B\right)
            -
            4n^2\log\left(2+\frac{q}{n^2}\right)
        \right]
    \right\}.
\end{equation}
In particular, for any fixed inverse temperature $\beta>0$, along any family of
parameters satisfying
\begin{equation}
    q\gg n^2\log\left(2+\frac{q}{n^2}\right),
\end{equation}
the memory time of $Z$-error Metropolis dynamics is exponential in $n$.
<!-- end prop-13 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Given in Appendix \ref{app:proofOBCOBCHGP}. The proof is similar to the classical case: we first lift the code to $\mathbb Z$ and define a generalized cylinder with some gauge-invariant quantities for each codeword in the $Z$-error sector. The generalized cylinder is exponentially long, so that reaching a cap requires exponentially many local updates. Reaching the side of the cylinder is suppressed by the large energy it costs. Consequently, the side is reached with exponentially small probability within an exponentially long time. Reduction modulo $q$ folds the cylinder into $\mathbb Z_q^N$, but the cylinder does not intersect itself and the energy of every configuration inside the cylinder is also preserved, so its boundary remains a valid bottleneck, which gives the memory-time bound \eqref{eq:tmixOBCOBCHGP}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- eg-14: body_md -->
\label{eg:highTmemory}
Consider a stabilizer code on two $q$-dimensional qudits and one qubit, with Hilbert space $\mathbb{C}^q \otimes \mathbb{C}^q \otimes \mathbb{C}^2$. Let $q$ be odd, and let $X_i$ and $Z_i$ denote the Pauli operators on the $i$-th tensor factor. Consider the stabilizer code generated by
\begin{equation}
S_X=X_1X_2,\qquad S_Z=Z_1Z_2^{-1}.
\end{equation}
This code encodes one qubit, with $X_3$ and $Z_3$ as logical $X$ and logical $Z$, respectively. Take the elementary physical errors to be
\begin{equation}
E_1=X_1X_3,\qquad E_2=Z_1Z_3,\qquad E_3=X_2X_3,\qquad E_4=Z_2Z_3.
\end{equation}
We define the weight of an operator as the minimum number of elementary physical errors required to generate it, up to phase. Since
\begin{equation}
S_X=E_1E_3,\qquad S_Z=E_2E_4^{-1},
\end{equation}
both stabilizer generators have weight two. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
E_1^q=E_3^q=X_3,\qquad E_2^q=E_4^q=Z_3,
\end{equation}
and it can be checked that the distance of the code is $q$.
<!-- end eg-14 -->
<!-- prop-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:highTmemory}
    The code of Example~\ref{eg:highTmemory} is a high-temperature memory for the elementary physical errors $E_1,\ldots,E_4$. For any $\varepsilon>0$, if \begin{equation}
        \frac{1}{\beta} < \frac{q}{\log^{1+\varepsilon}q}, \label{eq:beta_q_epsilon}
    \end{equation}
    then for sufficiently large $q$ the memory time is parametrically longer than it is at infinite temperature. In particular, the memory time scales as \begin{equation}\label{eq:pathology}
        t_{\mathrm{mem}} = \mathrm{\Theta}\left(  \frac{\mathrm{e}^{\beta (q-1)/2}}{2(q-1)}\right).
    \end{equation}
<!-- end prop-14 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
We can bound the memory time assuming that we undergo random $E_{1,2,3,4}^\pm 1$ errors with Metropolis sampling rates, using the same bottleneck theorem as in Proposition \ref{prop:PBCslowmixing}.  Consider the set of errors of total ``Lee weight'' exactly equal to $\frac{q-1}{2}$.   We may use the stabilizers to reduce the error set to only $E_1$ and $E_2$ up to stabilizer-equivalent errors.   There are then exactly $2(q-1)$ such errors, as one may easily count.  The Gibbs probability of this set of error configurations is \begin{equation}
        \pi_\beta(\text{bottleneck}) = \frac{1}{Z(\beta)} \cdot 2(q-1)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta (q-1)/2}.
    \end{equation}
    The Markov chain bottleneck theorem~\cite[Thm.~7.4]{LevinPeresWilmer2017} implies \eqref{eq:pathology}.

    At $\beta=0$, there is no bottleneck, and the mixing time is algebraic:  $t_{\mathrm{mem}} = \mathrm{\Theta}(q^2)$ is limited by the random walk time across a two-dimensional torus of $E_{1,2}$ errors.  When $\beta$ obeys  \eqref{eq:beta_q_epsilon}, the memory time grows faster than any power law in $q$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:A1}
    $\mathcal{C}=\ker_{\mathbb{Z}_q} (H_X)/\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_Z^{\mathsf{T}}) \cong \ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1)/\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_2)$.
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
To show the equivalence between these two spaces, we proceed as follows: \begin{subequations}\begin{align}
        \ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1) &= \left\lbrace \left(\begin{array}{c} z \\ t\end{array}\right) \in \mathbb{Z}^{n+m_X} : H_Xz=qt \right\rbrace , \label{eq:kerZD1} \\
        \mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_2) &= \left\lbrace \left(\begin{array}{c} H_Z^{\mathsf{T}}a+qb \\ q^{-1}H_XH_Z^{\mathsf{T}}a+H_Xb\end{array}\right) \in \mathbb{Z}^{n+m_X} : \left(\begin{array}{c} a \\ b\end{array}\right) \in \mathbb{Z}^{m_Z+n}  \right\rbrace . \label{eq:imZD2}
    \end{align}\end{subequations}
    Define the projection $\pi : \mathbb{Z}^{n+m_X} \rightarrow \mathbb{Z}_q^n$ via \begin{equation}
        \pi(z,t)  =z \; \text{(mod $q$)}.
    \end{equation}
    It follows immediately from \eqref{eq:kerZD1} that $\pi(\ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1)) = \ker_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_X)$, and from \eqref{eq:imZD2} that $\pi(\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_2)) = \mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_Z^{\mathsf{T}})$.    Therefore there is an induced homomorphism $\overline{\pi}:  \ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1)/\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_2) \rightarrow \ker_{\mathbb{Z}_q} (H_X)/\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_Z^{\mathsf{T}}) $.

To complete the proof, we show that \(\overline{\pi}\) is an isomorphism. To prove injectivity, suppose that the class of \((z,t)\in\ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1)\) is mapped by \(\overline{\pi}\) to the trivial class in \(\ker_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_X)/\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_Z^{\mathsf T})\). Then there exist \(a\in\mathbb{Z}^{m_Z}\) and \(b\in\mathbb{Z}^n\) such that
\begin{equation}
z=H_Z^{\mathsf T}a+qb.
\end{equation}
Since \(H_Xz=qt\), it follows that
\begin{equation}
t=q^{-1}H_XH_Z^{\mathsf T}a+H_Xb.
\end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation}
\begin{pmatrix}
z\\
t
\end{pmatrix}
=
D_2
\begin{pmatrix}
a\\
b
\end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
so \((z,t)\) is trivial in \(\ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1)/\mathrm{im}_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_2)\), and \(\overline{\pi}\) is injective. To prove surjectivity, take any element of \(\ker_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H_X)\) and choose an integer lift \(z\). Since \(H_Xz=0\pmod q\), there exists \(t\in\mathbb{Z}^{m_X}\) such that \(H_Xz=qt\). Hence \((z,t)\in\ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(D_1)\) and maps to the chosen element. Thus \(\overline{\pi}\) is an isomorphism.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:reduced_weight}
For any $(w_{\mathrm B},w_{\mathrm C})$-Lee-LDPC code, if $u\in\Lambda$ satisfies $|u|_2=\ell$, then the reduced Lee weight of the corresponding $X$-type
logical operator is at most
$\sqrt{n}\ell+\frac{w_{\mathrm C}}{2}n$. The same statement holds for every
$z\in\Lambda^*$.
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
For $u\in\Lambda$, choose $m\in\mathbb Z^{k_f}$ with
$u=(L_X^fP_X)^{\mathsf T}m$. The corresponding logical $X$ operator is then $x=(L_X^f)^{\mathsf T}m$. Since
$x-u\in\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H_X^{\mathsf T})$,
$x=u+S_X^{\mathsf T}\alpha$ for some real $\alpha$. Rounding $\alpha$ to the
nearest integer vector $\beta$, we obtain the vector $x-S_X^{\mathsf T}\beta$, which represents
the same logical operator and has Lee weight at most
$|u|_1+\frac{w_{\mathrm C}}{2}n$. Because
$|u|_1\le\sqrt n\,|u|_2=\sqrt n\,\ell$, the reduced Lee weight is at most
$\sqrt n\,\ell+\frac{w_{\mathrm C}}{2}n$. The proof for $z\in\Lambda^*$ is identical.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:transcendence_p}
    Let \(\Lambda\subset\mathbb R^k\) be a full-rank lattice, and let \(\Lambda^*\) be its Euclidean dual lattice.
    For $v\in \Lambda$, define \begin{equation}
        D_q(v) := \max_{0<a<q}\mathrm{dist}\left(\frac{av}{q}, \Lambda \right) = \max_{0<a<q}\left( \min_{\lambda\in \Lambda} \left|\frac{av}{q}-\lambda \right|_2 \right).
    \end{equation}
    If $v\in \Lambda \setminus q\Lambda$, or equivalently, $D_q(v)>0$, there exists a $w \in \Lambda^*$ such that $v\cdot w \not\mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0 \pmod{q}$ and \begin{equation}
       |w|_2 D_q(v) \le 2k.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
The proof is rather technical; we provide it in Appendix \ref{app:transcendence_p}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lem-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:smallDv}
    In the setting of Lemma~\ref{lem:transcendence_p}, if there exists $u\in \Lambda^*$ with $v\cdot u \not\mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0 \pmod{q}$, we can always find a vector $v'\in \Lambda$ with $v' \cdot u \not\mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0 \pmod{q}$ and $|v'|_2 \le 4D_q(v)$.
<!-- end lem-7 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
According to the definition of $D_q(v)$, for each $a=1,\ldots,q-1$, there exists $x_a \in \Lambda$ with $|x_a-\frac{av}{q}|_2\le D_q(v)$. We also set $x_0 = 0$ and $x_q=v$.
Define
\begin{equation}
    \Delta_a := x_{a+1}-2x_a+x_{a-1}, \quad d_a := x_{a+1}-x_a,
\end{equation}
so $|\Delta_a|_2\le 4D_q(v)$. We now show there is at least one $\Delta_a$ that has a nonzero pairing with $u$ modulo $q$. Suppose, on the contrary, that \begin{equation}
    \Delta_a \cdot u \mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0
    \quad (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
    \qquad
    \text{for all } a=1,\ldots,q-1.
\end{equation}
Since $\Delta_a =d_a-d_{a-1} $, all $d_a\cdot u$ are congruent modulo $q$. Let $c$ denote their common
residue. Summing over $a$ gives
\begin{equation}
    v\cdot u
    =
    (x_q-x_0)\cdot u
    =
    \sum_{a=0}^{q-1} d_a\cdot u
    \mathrel{\mathchar"3211}
    \sum_{a=0}^{q-1} c
    =
    qc
    \mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0
    \quad (\mathrm{mod}\ q),
\end{equation}
which contradicts $v\cdot u \not\mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0 \pmod{q}$.
Thus $\Delta_a\cdot u\not\mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0 \pmod{q}$ for some $a$.
Taking $v':=\Delta_a$, we obtain $v'\in\Lambda$,
$v'\cdot u\not\mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0 \pmod{q}$, and
$|v'|_2\le 4D_q(v)$, as claimed.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
Let $\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^k$ be a full-rank lattice, let
    $t\in\mathbb{R}^k$, and let $s>0$. If $r>s\sqrt{k/(2\pi)}$, then
    \begin{equation}
        \sum_{\substack{\lambda\in\Lambda:\\ |t+\lambda|_2\ge r}}
        \mathrm{e}^{-\pi |t+\lambda|_2^2/s^2}
        \le
        \rho_{s,\Lambda}(0)
        \mathrm{e}^{-
            \pi\left(
                r/s-\sqrt{k/(2\pi)}
            \right)^2}.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- defn-9: body_md -->
A quantum non-CSS stabilizer code with stabilizer matrix
$H\in\mathbb Z_q^{m\times 2n}$ is a $(w_B,w_C)$-Lee-LDPC code if
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{j=1}^{2n} |H_{ij}|_1 \le w_C
    \qquad
    \text{for each row } i,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{i=1}^{m} |H_{ij}|_1 \le w_B
    \qquad
    \text{for each column } j.
\end{equation}
<!-- end defn-9 -->
<!-- prop-15: body_md -->
\label{prop:HJH0} For any quantum \((w_B,w_C)\)-Lee-LDPC non-CSS stabilizer code over \(\mathbb Z_q\), if \(q>w_C^2\), then, using the integer lift convention above,
\(
HJH^{\mathsf T}=0
\)
over \(\mathbb Z\).
<!-- end prop-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Each entry of \(HJH^{\mathsf T}\) is the symplectic pairing of two rows of \(H\), and hence has absolute value at most \(w_C^2\). Stabilizer commutation implies that it vanishes modulo \(q\), so for \(q>w_C^2\) it must vanish over \(\mathbb Z\).
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lem-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:nonCSSdecomposition}
Consider any stabilizer code with stabilizer matrix $H$. Let $UHV=D=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,\ldots,d_r,0,\ldots,0)$ be the Smith normal
form of $H$ over $\mathbb Z$, and let $g_i=\gcd(d_i,q)$. If $H J H^\mathsf{T}=0$ over $\mathbb{Z}$, there exist dual logical bases of $\mathcal C$, written as
\begin{equation}
    \{u_1^f,\ldots,u_{k_f}^f,\ u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\}_{i:g_i>1}
    \qquad\text{and}\qquad
    \{v_1^f,\ldots,v_{k_f}^f,\ v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\}_{i:g_i>1},
\end{equation}
with $k_f=n-r$, such that
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal C
    \cong
    \bigoplus_{\mu=1}^{k_f}
    \left(
        \mathbb Z_q u_\mu^f
        \oplus
        \mathbb Z_q v_\mu^f
    \right)
    \oplus
    \bigoplus_{i:g_i>1}
    \left(
        \mathbb Z_{g_i}u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}
        \oplus
        \mathbb Z_{g_i}v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}
    \right).
\end{equation}
In particular, $\mathcal C$ splits into a free part of rank $2k_f$ and a torsion
part whose nontrivial blocks are indexed precisely by the Smith factors
satisfying $g_i>1$, and the only nonzero pairings of the dual bases are
\begin{equation} \label{eq:noncsspairings}
    \left\langle u_\mu^f, v_\nu^f \right\rangle
    =
    \delta_{\mu\nu},
    \qquad
    \left\langle u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    =
    \frac{q}{g_i}\delta_{ij}.
\end{equation}
Here, as in Appendix~\ref{app:baddistance}, ``free'' and ``torsion'' refer to the integer decomposition. A block with $g_i=q$ is counted as torsion even though it is not torsion in the sense of Section~\ref{sec:css}.

Moreover, if $H$ is Lee-LDPC, then the dual bases may be chosen so that for
every torsion pair
\begin{equation}
    \left(u_i^{\mathrm{tor}},v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\right),
\end{equation}
at least one of the two logical classes admits a representative of reduced Lee
weight $O(n)$.
<!-- end lem-9 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
First, we take the Smith normal form of $H$ and write
\begin{equation}
    UHV = D = \operatorname{diag}(d_1,\ldots,d_r,0,\ldots,0).
\end{equation}
We define $v_i$ to be the $i$-th column of $V$, and $w_i$ to be the $i$-th column of $(V^{-1})^{\mathsf T}$. 
Let
\begin{equation}
S=\operatorname{Sat}\!\left(\operatorname{im}(H^{\mathsf{T}})\right)=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Z}\{w_1,\ldots,w_r\}.
\end{equation}
Since $d_iw_i\in\operatorname{im}(H^{\mathsf T})$ for $i\le r$ and $HJH^{\mathsf{T}}=0$ over $\mathbb{Z}$, we have $d_id_j\langle w_i,w_j\rangle=0$, so $S$ is a primitive isotropic sublattice of the unimodular symplectic lattice $\mathbb{Z}^{2n}$. Therefore, the basis $w_1,\ldots,w_r$ of $S$ can be extended to an integral symplectic basis of $\mathbb{Z}^{2n}$. We choose this extension so that
\begin{equation}
\ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(HJ)=S\oplus \mathcal W,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
\mathcal W=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}}\{w_{r+1},\ldots,w_{2n-r}\},
\end{equation}
and, with $k_f=n-r$,
\begin{equation}
\{w_{r+1},\ldots,w_{r+k_f},w_{r+k_f+1},\ldots,w_{2n-r}\}
\end{equation}
is a Darboux basis of $\mathcal W$. After choosing this extension, redefine \(V\) so that \(w_i\) is the \(i\)-th column of \((V^{-1})^{\mathsf T}\) for all \(i\). Since the first \(r\) vectors \(w_1,\ldots,w_r\) are unchanged, \(UHV=D\) still holds.
Therefore,
\begin{subequations}
\begin{equation}
\left\langle w_{r+\mu},w_{r+\nu}\right\rangle=0,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\left\langle w_{r+k_f+\mu},w_{r+k_f+\nu}\right\rangle=0,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\left\langle w_{r+\mu},w_{r+k_f+\nu}\right\rangle=\delta_{\mu\nu},
\end{equation}
\end{subequations}

We now fix the part of the torsion basis that we will need
later.
For each $i$ satisfying $g_i>1$, we directly set
\begin{equation}
    u_i^{\mathrm{tor}} := w_i\in\mathcal C.
\end{equation}

These vectors will serve as one side of the torsion basis. We make this choice because $d_iw_i$ lies in $\operatorname{im}(H^{\mathsf T})$, so the order of $u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}$ is exactly controlled by $g_i$. In particular, $\left\langle u_i^{\mathrm{tor}},u_j^{\mathrm{tor}}\right\rangle=0$ because $S$ is isotropic.
Moreover, $w_i$ lies in the real span of
$H^{\mathsf T}$, and this is the source of the later $O(n)$ weight bound.

To choose the other side of the torsion basis, we first define the raw torsion
vectors
\begin{equation}
    \tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}
    :=
    \frac{q}{g_i}\,v_iJ.
\end{equation}

These vectors define logical operators, because
\begin{equation}
    HJ(\tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}})^{\mathsf T}
    =
    \frac{q}{g_i}\,Hv_i^{\mathsf T}
    =
    \frac{q}{g_i}\,d_i(U^{-1}e_i)
    =
    0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q).
\end{equation}

Moreover, they pair with the previously fixed torsion vectors
$u_j^{\mathrm{tor}}=w_j$ as
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle u_j^{\mathrm{tor}},\tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\right\rangle
    =
    \frac{q}{g_i}\delta_{ij}.
\end{equation}

We next modify the entire $\tilde v^{\mathrm{tor}}$-block, while keeping the
whole $u^{\mathrm{tor}}$-block fixed. Since $\tilde v_j^{\mathrm{tor}}$ has
order $g_j$ in $\mathcal C$, we write
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle \tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, \tilde v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    =
    c_{ij}\frac{q}{g_j}
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q),
    \qquad
    i<j.
\end{equation}

Using these coefficients, we replace the raw torsion vectors by
\begin{equation}
    v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}
    :=
    \tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}
    -
    \sum_{j>i} c_{ij}u_j^{\mathrm{tor}}.
\end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle u_k^{\mathrm{tor}}, v_i^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    =
    \left\langle u_k^{\mathrm{tor}}, \tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    =
    \frac{q}{g_i}\delta_{ki}.
\end{equation}

On the other hand, for every $i<j$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    \left\langle v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    &=
    \left\langle \tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, \tilde v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    -
    c_{ij}
    \left\langle u_j^{\mathrm{tor}}, \tilde v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle \\
    &=
    \left\langle \tilde v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, \tilde v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle
    -
    c_{ij}\frac{q}{g_j}
    \mathrel{\mathchar"3211} 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

Since the symplectic pairing is alternating, we also have
\(
    \left\langle v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, v_i^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle = 0
\).
Therefore
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}, v_j^{\mathrm{tor}} \right\rangle = 0
\end{equation}
for all $i,j$.
Thus the torsion sector is organized into dual pairs
\begin{equation}
    \left(u_i^{\mathrm{tor}},v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\right),
    \qquad
    g_i>1.
\end{equation}



We now come to the free part. 
We choose the free logical basis from the Darboux basis of $\mathcal W$. For $\mu=1,\ldots,k_f$, define
\begin{equation}
u_\mu^f=w_{r+\mu},\qquad v_\mu^f=w_{r+k_f+\mu}.
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal W\subseteq\ker_{\mathbb{Z}}(HJ)$, all these vectors are integer logical operators. By the choice of the Darboux basis,
\begin{equation}
\left\langle u_\mu^f,u_\nu^f\right\rangle
=
\left\langle v_\mu^f,v_\nu^f\right\rangle
=0,
\qquad
\left\langle u_\mu^f,v_\nu^f\right\rangle
=\delta_{\mu\nu}.
\end{equation}

Since \(\mathcal W \subseteq S^{\perp_J}\), both free blocks pair trivially with every
\(u_i^{\mathrm{tor}} \in S\). Moreover, for every \(a>r\),
\begin{equation}
\left\langle w_a,v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\right\rangle
=
\frac{q}{g_i}w_a\cdot v_i
= 0.
\end{equation}
Therefore, both free blocks also pair trivially with the adjusted vectors
\(v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\). The only pairings among these generators that can be nonzero are those given in \eqref{eq:noncsspairings}.

The pairing relations show that the free generators have order \(q\), the torsion generators indexed by \(i\) have order \(g_i\), and these generators are independent in \(\mathcal C\). Hence they generate a subgroup of order $q^{2k_f}\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_i^2$.
Since \(J\) is unimodular, \(HJ\) has the same Smith factors as \(H\). Therefore,
\begin{equation}
|\mathcal C|
=
\frac{\left|\ker_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(HJ)\right|}
{\left|\operatorname{im}_{\mathbb{Z}_q}(H^{\mathsf T})\right|}
=
q^{2(n-r)}\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_i^2
=
q^{2k_f}\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_i^2.
\end{equation}
The constructed generators therefore form the claimed basis of \(\mathcal C\).

Now assume that $H$ is the stabilizer matrix of a $(w_B,w_C)$-Lee-LDPC code. For every $i$ with
$g_i>1$, the vector $u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}=w_i$ lies in
$\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})$. Hence, after subtracting a
suitable integer linear combination of stabilizers, we may choose a representative
of the same logical class whose stabilizer coefficients all lie in
$[-1/2,1/2]$. Its reduced Lee weight is therefore at most one half of the total
Lee weight of $r$ linearly independent rows of $H$ spanning $\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})$. Since each such row has Lee weight at
most $w_C$, this gives
\begin{equation}
    \left|u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\right|_L
    \le
    \frac{w_C}{2}r
    \le
    \frac{w_C}{2}n.
\end{equation}
Therefore, in every torsion pair
\begin{equation}
    \left(u_i^{\mathrm{tor}},v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}\right),
    \qquad
    \gcd(d_i,q)>1,
\end{equation}
one of the two logical classes admits a representative of reduced Lee weight at
most $\frac{w_C}{2}n$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- thm-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:noncsswitness}
For any quantum $(w_B,w_C)$-Lee-LDPC non-CSS stabilizer code on $\mathbb Z_q$,
let $L_1,L_2\in\mathcal C$ be nontrivial noncommuting logical operators, namely
\begin{equation}
    \langle L_1,L_2\rangle \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q).
\end{equation}
Then there must exist either
$\widetilde L_1\in\mathcal C$ or $\widetilde L_2\in\mathcal C$ such that
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle \widetilde L_1,L_2\right\rangle \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q)
    \qquad
    \left[
    \text{or }
    \left\langle L_1,\widetilde L_2\right\rangle \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q)
    \right],
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}
    \min\left(
        |\widetilde L_1|_{\mathrm L}^{\mathrm{red}},
        |\widetilde L_2|_{\mathrm L}^{\mathrm{red}}
    \right)
    \le Cn,
\end{equation}
where $C$ is bounded independently of $n$ and $q$:
\begin{equation} \label{eq:noncssconstant}
C \le
\begin{cases}
w_C^2,
&
q\le w_C^2,
\\[0.4em]
4\sqrt{2}+\dfrac{1}{2}w_C,
&
q>w_C^2.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
<!-- end thm-7 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
When $q\le w_C^2$, the proof is trivial. For $q>w_C^2$, Porposition \ref{prop:HJH0} gives $H J H^\mathsf{T}=0$ over $\mathbb{Z}$, so we can apply Lemma~\ref{lem:nonCSSdecomposition} to $L_1$ and $L_2$.
We first treat the case in which one of the two logical operators has a nonzero
component along the torsion $v$-block. If, for some $i$ with $g_i>1$,
the coefficient of $v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}$ in $L_1$ is nonzero, then by the
construction of the dual bases,
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle u_i^{\mathrm{tor}},L_1\right\rangle \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q).
\end{equation}

The same conclusion holds with $L_2$ in place of $L_1$. On the other hand,
Lemma \ref{lem:nonCSSdecomposition} shows that the logical class of $u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}$ admits a
representative of reduced Lee weight at most $\frac{w_C}{2}n$. Therefore, unless
one of the two cases above already gives the desired $O(n)$ upper bound, we may
assume that both $L_1$ and $L_2$ have zero component along every torsion vector
$v_i^{\mathrm{tor}}$. Under this assumption, the torsion part cannot contribute
to their pairing, so the nonzero pairing between $L_1$ and $L_2$ must come
entirely from the free part. We now restrict to this residual case, and write the chosen noncommuting logical pair as $L_1^{\mathrm{res}}$ and $L_2^{\mathrm{res}}$. We take each $L_j^{\mathrm{res}}$ to be the integer vector $\sum_\mu(a_\mu u_\mu^f+b_\mu v_\mu^f)+\sum_i c_iu_i^{\mathrm{tor}}$, with integer coefficients depending on $j$. Since every $u_i^{\mathrm{tor}}$ lies in $S\subset\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})$, the projection $P$ defined below annihilates it, so $PL_j^{\mathrm{res}}$ lies in the lattice $\Lambda$ defined below.

Choose $r$ linearly independent rows of $H$ that span its real row space, and let $B$ be the matrix formed by these rows, so every row of $B$ has Lee weight at most $w_C$. Define
\begin{equation}
    P = I - B^{\mathsf T}(BB^{\mathsf T})^{-1}B.
\end{equation}
Thus $PL_1^{\mathrm{res}}$ and $PL_2^{\mathrm{res}}$ are obtained by projecting $L_1^{\mathrm{res}}$ and $L_2^{\mathrm{res}}$ onto
$\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})^\perp$, and this projection preserves their pairing:
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle PL_1^{\mathrm{res}},PL_2^{\mathrm{res}}\right\rangle
    =
    \left\langle L_1^{\mathrm{res}},L_2^{\mathrm{res}}\right\rangle
    \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q).
\end{equation}

Let
\begin{equation}
    \Lambda
    :=
    P\,\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Z}
    \{u_1^f,\ldots,u_{k_f}^f,v_1^f,\ldots,v_{k_f}^f\}.
\end{equation}
Because \(\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})\subseteq\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)\), the restriction of \(P\) to \(\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)\) has kernel \(\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})\) and image \(\ker_{\mathbb R}(H)\cap\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)\). Therefore, this real space has dimension \(2n-2r=2k_f\). Moreover, \(P\) preserves the symplectic pairing on \(\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)\): for every \(x\in\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)\), the difference \(x-Px\) lies in \(\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb R}(H^{\mathsf T})\), which pairs trivially with \(\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)\). The full pairing relations in \eqref{eq:noncsspairings} therefore show that the projected free basis is a Darboux basis, so \(\Lambda\) is a full-rank lattice in this real space. This real space is also \(J\)-invariant. Since all symplectic pairings in \(\Lambda\) are integral, \(\Lambda J\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathrm{Euc}}^*\). The matrix of Euclidean pairings between the projected free basis and its \(J\)-image is, up to sign, the standard symplectic matrix and is therefore unimodular. Hence the Euclidean dual lattice of \(\Lambda\) is
\begin{equation} \label{eq:noncssdual}
    \Lambda_{\mathrm{Euc}}^*=\Lambda J.
\end{equation}

We apply Lemmas~\ref{lem:transcendence_p} and \ref{lem:smallDv} within the $2k_f$-dimensional space $\ker_{\mathbb R}(H)\cap\ker_{\mathbb R}(HJ)$, so $k=2k_f$. Now consider the projected residual vector $PL_1^{\mathrm{res}}\in\Lambda$. If
\(
    D_q(PL_1^{\mathrm{res}})
    \le
    \sqrt{k_f}
\),
then Lemma~\ref{lem:smallDv}, applied with $u:=PL_2^{\mathrm{res}}J^{\mathsf T}\in\Lambda_{\mathrm{Euc}}^*$, for which $PL_1^{\mathrm{res}}\cdot u=\langle PL_1^{\mathrm{res}},PL_2^{\mathrm{res}}\rangle\not\equiv0 \pmod{q}$, gives a vector $W\in\Lambda$ such that
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle W,PL_2^{\mathrm{res}}\right\rangle
    \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    |W|_2\le 4\sqrt{k_f}.
\end{equation}

On the other hand, if
$D_q(PL_1^{\mathrm{res}})\ge \sqrt{k_f}$, then, since
$\Lambda_{\mathrm{Euc}}^*=\Lambda J$, Lemma~\ref{lem:transcendence_p} gives a vector
$Y\in\Lambda J$ such that
\begin{equation}
    PL_1^{\mathrm{res}}\cdot Y\ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    |Y|_2D_q(PL_1^{\mathrm{res}})
    \le
    4k_f.
\end{equation}
Write $Y=WJ$ with $W\in\Lambda$. Then
\begin{equation}
    \left\langle PL_1^{\mathrm{res}},W\right\rangle
    \ne 0
    \quad(\mathrm{mod}\ q)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
    |W|_2=|Y|_2\le 4\sqrt{k_f}.
\end{equation}
Thus $W$ gives a short noncommuting witness for $L_1^{\mathrm{res}}$.

In either case, $W$ corresponds to a logical operator $\widetilde L\in\mathcal C$
whose reduced Lee weight satisfies
\begin{equation} \label{eq:noncssreducedweight}
    |\widetilde L|_{\mathrm L}^{\mathrm{red}}
    \le
    4\sqrt{2nk_f}
    +
    \frac{w_C}{2}n \le \left(4\sqrt{2}+\frac{w_C}{2}\right)n,
\end{equation}
by the same rounding argument as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:reduced_weight}.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lem-10: body_md -->
For every fixed $\beta>0$, there exist $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)>0$ and
$C_{a,b}(\beta)>0$ such that
\begin{equation} \label{eq:RPFtheorem}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m 1\right)(0)
    =
    C_{a,b}(\beta)\rho_{a,b}(\beta)^m[1+o(1)]
\end{equation}
as $m\to\infty$.
<!-- end lem-10 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
We apply the Ruelle--Perron--Frobenius theorem for contractive iterated function systems. It applies to a finite family of $C^1$ strict contractions on a compact convex set $K$, provided that the derivatives never vanish and the weights are strictly positive and Dini-continuous. The theorem states that, after normalization by the corresponding power of the spectral radius, the iterates converge uniformly on $K$ to a nonzero positive rank-one limit. We now show that this theorem applies to $\mathcal L_\beta$.

Let
\begin{equation}
    K
    =
    \left[
        0,\frac{a-1}{a-b}
    \right].
\end{equation}

For every $t\in K$ and $s\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$,
\begin{equation}
    0
    \le
    \frac{bt+s}{a}
    \le
    \frac{b(a-1)/(a-b)+a-1}{a}
    =
    \frac{a-1}{a-b}.
\end{equation}

Thus for each $s\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}$, the map $t\longmapsto \frac{bt+s}{a}$ sends $K$ into itself. The images of $K$ under these maps form a chain of overlapping intervals from
$0$ to $(a-1)/(a-b)$, since $b(a-1)/(a-b)\ge 1$. Their union is therefore $K$.

For any $t,t'\in K$ and any $s$, the branch $t\mapsto(bt+s)/a$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \left|
        \frac{bt+s}{a}
        -
        \frac{bt'+s}{a}
    \right|
    =
    \frac{b}{a}|t-t'|.
\end{equation}
Since $a>b$, each map is a strict contraction with contraction ratio $b/a<1$. Each map is $C^1$, and its derivative is $b/a>0$, so the derivative never vanishes.

For the map corresponding to $s$, the weight is $G_\beta((bt+s)/a) / G_\beta(0)$. As a function of $t$, this weight is strictly positive and analytic on $K$, and is therefore Dini-continuous. The Ruelle--Perron--Frobenius theorem therefore applies to $\mathcal L_\beta$. Let $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)$ be the spectral radius of $\mathcal L_\beta$. The theorem gives $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)>0$ and shows that $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)^{-m}\mathcal L_\beta^m 1$ converges uniformly on $K$ to a strictly positive continuous function. Let $C_{a,b}(\beta)>0$ be the value of this limiting function at $0$. Evaluating the uniform convergence at $0$ gives \eqref{eq:RPFtheorem}.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lem-11: body_md -->
For every $\beta>0$,\begin{equation}
    1<\rho_{a,b}(\beta)<a.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-11 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
We first prove that $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)>1$. Separating the $s=0$ term in the
definition of $\mathcal L_\beta$ gives
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^{m+1}1\right)(0)
    -
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(0)
    =
    \sum_{s=1}^{a-1}
    \frac{G_\beta(s/a)}{G_\beta(0)}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(s/a).
\end{equation}

For each $s=1,\ldots,a-1$, retain in
$\left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(s/a)$ only the path that chooses the branch $0$ (i.e., $t\mapsto bt/a$) at
every subsequent step. Its argument after $j$ steps is
$sb^j/a^{j+1}$, and hence
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(s/a)
    \ge
    \prod_{j=1}^{m}
    \frac{G_\beta(sb^j/a^{j+1})}{G_\beta(0)}
    \ge
    \prod_{j=1}^{\infty}
    \frac{G_\beta(sb^j/a^{j+1})}{G_\beta(0)}.
\end{equation}
The second inequality follows from
$0<G_\beta(t)/G_\beta(0)\le 1$. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
    0
    \le
    1-\frac{G_\beta(t)}{G_\beta(0)}
    =
    \frac{1-\cos(2\pi t)}
    {\cosh\beta-\cos(2\pi t)}
    \le
    \frac{2\pi^2t^2}{\cosh\beta-1},
\end{equation}
and therefore
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{j=1}^{\infty}
    \left[
        1-
        \frac{G_\beta(sb^j/a^{j+1})}{G_\beta(0)}
    \right]
    \le
    \frac{2\pi^2s^2}{a^2(\cosh\beta-1)}
    \sum_{j=1}^{\infty}
    \left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^{2j}
    <
    \infty.
\end{equation}

The corresponding infinite-product criterion can be verified directly. Since
$sb^j/a^{j+1}\to 0$, the factors converge to $1$ and are therefore eventually
at least $1/2$. For $1/2\le x\le 1$,
\begin{equation}
    -\log x\le 2(1-x).
\end{equation}
Thus the sum of the negative logarithms of these factors is finite. The finitely
many preceding factors are also strictly positive, so the logarithm of the
infinite product does not tend to negative infinity. Consequently,
\begin{equation}
    \prod_{j=1}^{\infty}
    \frac{G_\beta(sb^j/a^{j+1})}{G_\beta(0)}
    >
    0.
\end{equation}

Substituting this bound into the increment above, with the prefactor
$G_\beta(s/a)/G_\beta(0)$ supplying the $j=0$ factor, gives
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^{m+1}1\right)(0)
    -
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(0)
    \ge
    \sum_{s=1}^{a-1}
    \prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
    \frac{G_\beta(sb^j/a^{j+1})}{G_\beta(0)}
    >
    0.
\end{equation}

The right-hand side is independent of $m$. Iterating the inequality therefore
yields
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(0)
    \ge
    1
    +
    m
    \sum_{s=1}^{a-1}
    \prod_{j=0}^{\infty}
    \frac{G_\beta(sb^j/a^{j+1})}{G_\beta(0)}
    \longrightarrow
    \infty.
\end{equation}

If $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)<1$, then by \eqref{eq:RPFtheorem} $(\mathcal L_\beta^m1)(0)\to0$,
whereas if $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)=1$, it tends to $C_{a,b}(\beta)$. Both cases
contradict the preceding divergence. Hence
\begin{equation}
    \rho_{a,b}(\beta)>1.
\end{equation}

We now prove that $\rho_{a,b}(\beta)<a$. Because $\sum_{s=0}^{a-1}e^{2\pi iks/a}$ vanishes unless $a\mid k$, we have
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    \sum_{s=0}^{a-1}
    G_\beta\!\left(\frac{bt+s}{a}\right)
    &=
    \sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}
    e^{-\beta|k|}
    e^{2\pi i kbt/a}
    \sum_{s=0}^{a-1}
    e^{2\pi i ks/a}
    \\
    &=
    a\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}
    e^{-a\beta|k|}
    e^{2\pi i kbt}
    =
    aG_{a\beta}(bt).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

Because $G_{a\beta}$ is maximized at $0$, we have
\begin{equation}
    \sum_{s=0}^{a-1}
    \frac{G_\beta((bt+s)/a)}{G_\beta(0)}
    =
    a\frac{G_{a\beta}(bt)}{G_\beta(0)}
    \le
    a\frac{\coth(a\beta/2)}{\coth(\beta/2)}.
\end{equation}

This row-sum bound implies inductively that, for every $m\ge 0$,
\begin{equation}
    \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(t)
    \le
    \left[
        a\frac{\coth(a\beta/2)}{\coth(\beta/2)}
    \right]^m.
\end{equation}
Therefore, \begin{equation}
    \rho_{a,b}(\beta)
    =
    \lim_{m\to\infty}
    \left[
        \left(\mathcal L_\beta^m1\right)(0)
    \right]^{1/m}
    \le
    a\frac{\coth(a\beta/2)}{\coth(\beta/2)}
    <
    a.\qedhere
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lem-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:SMB}
Define the left shift $\sigma$ on $\{0,\ldots,a-1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}$ by $(\sigma u)_j=u_{j+1}$. 
The distribution of $B$ is stationary if
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{P}_\beta(B\in\sigma^{-1}E)=\mathbb{P}_\beta(B\in E)
\end{equation}
for every measurable subset $E\subseteq\{0,\ldots,a-1\}^{\mathbb{Z}}$.
A stationary distribution is ergodic if every measurable subset $E$ satisfying $\sigma^{-1}E=E$
also satisfies
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{P}_\beta(B\in E)\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation}
Suppose that the distribution of $B$ is stationary and ergodic. For every
\begin{equation}
u=(u_1,\ldots,u_d)\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}^d,
\end{equation}
define
\begin{equation}
p_d(u)=\mathbb{P}_\beta(B_1=u_1,\ldots,B_d=u_d).
\end{equation}
The Shannon entropy of the length-$d$ block is
\begin{equation}
H(B_1,\ldots,B_d)=-\sum_{u\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}^d}p_d(u)\log p_d(u),
\end{equation}
and the entropy rate of $B$ is
\begin{equation}
h_\beta=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}H(B_1,\ldots,B_d).
\end{equation}
For every $\eta>0$, define
\begin{equation}
A_d=\left\{u\in\{0,\ldots,a-1\}^d:p_d(u)\ge e^{-d(h_\beta+\eta)}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{P}_\beta\bigl((B_1,\ldots,B_d)\in A_d\bigr)\longrightarrow1,
\qquad |A_d|\le e^{d(h_\beta+\eta)}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lem-12 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
The Shannon--McMillan--Breiman theorem gives
\begin{equation}
-\frac{1}{d}\log p_d(B_1,\ldots,B_d)\xrightarrow[d\to\infty]{\mathrm{a.s.}}h_\beta.
\end{equation}
By the definition of $A_d$, $(B_1,\ldots,B_d)\in A_d$
is equivalent to
\begin{equation}
-\frac{1}{d}\log p_d(B_1,\ldots,B_d)\le h_\beta+\eta.
\end{equation}
Almost-sure convergence therefore implies that this event eventually occurs for almost every realization, and hence
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{P}_\beta\bigl((B_1,\ldots,B_d)\in A_d\bigr)\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}
Moreover, every $u\in A_d$ has probability at least $e^{-d(h_\beta+\eta)}$. Therefore,
\begin{equation}
1\ge\sum_{u\in A_d}p_d(u)\ge|A_d|e^{-d(h_\beta+\eta)},
\end{equation}
which gives the stated cardinality bound.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lem-13: body_md -->
There exist integer-valued random variables $(C_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$ and digits $(B_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$ satisfying $bC_j-y_j=aC_{j-1}+B_j$, where $B_j\in\{0,1,\ldots,a-1\}$, such that the distribution of $B=(B_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$ is stationary and ergodic. Moreover, $C_j$ is independent of $(y_k)_{k\le j}$ for every $j$, and $h_\beta<\log a$.
<!-- end lem-13 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Viewed from right to left, the carries $C_j$ form a Markov chain. Given $C_j=c$, sample $y_j$ independently with the distribution above and set
\begin{equation}
C_{j-1}=\left\lfloor\frac{bc-y_j}{a}\right\rfloor,\qquad B_j=bc-y_j-aC_{j-1}.
\end{equation}
For any $c,c'\in\mathbb{Z}$, choosing $y_j=bc-ac'$ gives $C_{j-1}=c'$, so the chain is irreducible; taking $c'=c$ also gives a self-loop. Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}_\beta\!\left[|C_{j-1}|-|C_j|\mid C_j=c\right]\le-\left(1-\frac{b}{a}\right)|c|+\frac{\mathbb{E}_\beta|y_j|}{a}+1.
\end{equation}
Since $\beta>0$, the distribution of $y_j$ has exponentially decaying tails, and hence $\mathbb{E}_\beta|y_j|<\infty$. Since $b<a$, the right-hand side is at most $-1$ for all sufficiently large $|c|$. The Foster--Lyapunov criterion therefore implies that the Markov chain is positive recurrent.

An irreducible positive recurrent Markov chain has a unique stationary distribution. Let $\mu$ denote this stationary distribution. On any finite interval of indices, sample the carry at the right endpoint according to $\mu$, independently sample the $y_j$ on that interval, and apply the recursion from right to left. The invariance of $\mu$ under the carry transition and the independence of the $y_j$ make these finite-dimensional distributions consistent under restriction and invariant under translations of the indices. By the Kolmogorov extension theorem, they define a stationary bi-infinite joint process $(C_j,y_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$. We continue to denote its distribution and expectation by $\mathbb{P}_\beta$ and $\mathbb{E}_\beta$, respectively. By construction, the $y_j$ retain their original independent and identically distributed law, $C_j\sim\mu$ for every $j$, and $C_j$ is independent of $(y_k)_{k\le j}$.

The joint process $(C_j,y_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$, read from right to left, is a stationary Markov chain on $\mathbb{Z}^2$. Starting from $(C_j,y_j)=(c,y)$, the recursion fixes $C_{j-1}$. For any prescribed state $(c',y')\in\mathbb{Z}^2$, choosing $y_{j-1}=bC_{j-1}-ac'$ and $y_{j-2}=y'$ gives $(C_{j-2},y_{j-2})=(c',y')$. Both choices have positive probability because the distribution of each $y_j$ has full support on $\mathbb{Z}$. Hence the joint Markov chain is irreducible. A stationary irreducible Markov chain on a countable state space is ergodic with respect to the shift. Therefore, $(C_j,y_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$ is ergodic.

Each digit $B_j=bC_j-y_j-aC_{j-1}$ is obtained from the joint process by the same measurable rule at every index, so the distribution of $B=(B_j)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}$ is stationary. The inverse image of every shift-invariant measurable subset of digit sequences is shift-invariant for the joint process, so the distribution of $B$ is also ergodic.


For $d\ge 1$, define the weighted digit sum represented by a length-$d$ digit block by
\begin{equation}
V_d=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a^{d-j}b^{j-1}B_j.
\end{equation}
The map from $(B_1,\ldots,B_d)$ to $V_d\pmod{a^d}$ is bijective. Summing the carry identities over this block gives
\begin{equation}
V_d=b^dC_d-a^dC_0-\sum_{j=1}^d a^{d-j}b^{j-1}y_j.
\end{equation}
We next show that one finite digit block is not uniformly distributed. For a positive integer $L$, the term containing $C_0$ disappears after exponentiation because $C_0\in\mathbb{Z}$. The independence of $C_L$ from $y_1,\ldots,y_L$ then gives
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}_\beta e^{2\pi iV_L/a^L}
=\mathbb{E}_\beta e^{2\pi i(b/a)^LC_L}\prod_{j=1}^L\mathbb{E}_\beta e^{-2\pi ib^{j-1}y_j/a^j}.
\end{equation}
For every real $\theta$, the marginal distribution of $y_j$ gives $\mathbb{E}_\beta e^{i\theta y_j}=(\cosh\beta-1)/(\cosh\beta-\cos\theta)>0$. Thus every factor in the product is strictly positive. Since $C_L$ has distribution $\mu$,
\begin{equation}
\left|\mathbb{E}_\beta e^{2\pi i(b/a)^LC_L}-1\right|
\le\sum_{c\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu(c)\min\left\{2,2\pi\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^L|c|\right\}\longrightarrow 0.
\end{equation}
Hence $\mathbb{E}_\beta e^{2\pi iV_L/a^L}\neq 0$ for all sufficiently large $L$. Fix one such $L$. The residue $V_L\pmod{a^L}$ is not uniformly distributed. Using stationarity, subadditivity of block entropy, and the bijection between digit blocks and residues, we obtain
\begin{equation}
h_\beta\le\frac{1}{L}H(B_1,\ldots,B_L)=\frac{1}{L}H(V_L\bmod a^L)<\log a.
\end{equation}
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
